Distributional Theory and Statistical Inference for Linear Functions of Eigenvectors with Small Eigengaps
Joshua Agterberg

Abstract
Spectral methods have myriad applications in high-dimensional statistics and data science, and while previous works have primarily focused on ℓ 2 \ell_{2} or ℓ 2 , ∞ \ell_{2,\infty} eigenvector and singular vector perturbation theory, in many settings these analyses fall short of providing the fine-grained guarantees required for various inferential tasks. In this paper we study statistical inference for linear functions of eigenvectors and principal components with a particular emphasis on the setting where gaps between eigenvalues may be extremely small relative to the corresponding spiked eigenvalue, a regime which has been oft-neglected in the literature. It has been previously established that linear functions of eigenvectors and principal components incur a non-negligible bias, so in this work we provide Berry-Esseen bounds for empirical linear forms and their debiased counterparts respectively in the matrix denoising model and the spiked principal component analysis model, both under Gaussian noise. Next, we propose data-driven estimators for the appropriate bias and variance quantities resulting in approximately valid confidence intervals, and we demonstrate our theoretical results through numerical simulations. We further apply our results to obtain distributional theory and confidence intervals for eigenvector entries, for which debiasing is not necessary. Crucially, our proposed confidence intervals and bias-correction procedures can all be computed directly from data without sample-splitting and are asymptotically valid under minimal assumptions on the eigengap and signal strength. Furthermore, our Berry-Esseen bounds clearly reflect the effects of both signal strength and eigenvalue closeness on the estimation and inference tasks.



1
Introduction

Spectral methods, or algorithms and procedures that rely on eigenvectors, singular vectors, and related quantities, are a useful collection of tools for analyzing high-dimensional data, with applications to clustering
(
Löffler et al. 2021
)
, network analysis
(
Jin et al. 2023
;
Mao et al. 2020
)
, ranking
(
Chen et al. 2019
;
Chen et al. 2022
)
, principal component analysis
(
Johnstone and Lu 2009
)
, nonconvex optimization
(
Chi et al. 2019
)
, and tensor data analysis
(
Zhang and Xia 2018
)
, to name a few. With the growing ubiquity of spectral methods, there has been an increased interest in providing statistical analyses of these techniques that take into account both the structural properties of the problem at hand as well as the particularities of the noise, in some cases resulting in provably optimal guarantees for various spectral-based algorithms
(
Abbe et al. 2020
;
Abbe et al. 2022
;
Ling 2022
;
Nguyen and Zhang 2023
;
Zhang 2022
;
Zhang 2023
)
.

One common paradigm to analyze spectral methods is via classical deterministic perturbation theory, which studies the
\ell_{2}
errors between eigenvectors of some underlying matrix
\bm{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and the eigenvectors of a perturbed matrix
\bm{\widehat{M}}=\bm{M}+\bm{E}
as a function of the perturbation matrix
\bm{E}
and the eigenvalues or singular values of
\bm{M}
. By combining these techniques with tools from nonsaymptotic random matrix theory one can derive high-probability or expected
\ell_{2}
error rates for eigenvectors and singular vectors
(
Cai and Zhang 2018
)
. Furthermore, there has been a growing interest in
\ell_{2,\infty}
eigenspace and singular subspace perturbation theory
(
Cape et al. 2019b
)
,
which quantifies the entrywise and row-wise fluctuations of subspaces through careful probabilistic analysis of the noise. By carefully tailoring these analyses to the distributional properties of the noise matrix
\bm{E}
, estimation error rates can be obtained that go beyond what classical perturbation theory offers. In some cases, these entrywise guarantees can yield asymptotic normality for estimators of subspaces
(
Agterberg and Cape 2023
;
Cape et al. 2019a
)
, and these tools can be applied in various contexts, resulting in statistically sound inferential procedures
(
Chen et al. 2021b
;
Gao et al. 2023
;
Yan et al. 2021
)
.

However, in many contexts entrywise perturbation and distributional theory is still not sufficiently fine-grained to characterize the fluctuations of individual eigenvectors. Therefore, in this paper we contribute to the literature by studying statistical inference for a general linear function of a single eigenvector. Explicitly, given a pre-specified unit vector
\bm{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and an eigenvector of interest
\bm{u}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we study estimating the linear form
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
with the empirical eigenvector
\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
. There have been a few works in this area
(
Chen et al. 2021a
;
Cheng et al. 2021
;
Koltchinskii and Xia 2016
;
Koltchinskii and Lounici 2016
;
Koltchinskii et al. 2020
)
, though we provide a more thorough review of related work in
Section

5
.
One key downside of existing theory is that theoretical results often require highly pessimistic assumptions on the
eigengap
, the minimal separation between distinct eigenvalues. Therefore, in this work we contribute to the literature by studying statistical inference in regimes where the eigengap can be
significantly smaller
than previously assumed (possibly up to order
n^{-1/2}
smaller, ignoring logarithmic terms).

Our analysis is motivated by the findings of
Li et al. 2022
who provide perturbation bounds for estimators of
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
in the presence of small eigengaps. They show that a form of “debiasing” is necessary for the empirical estimate
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
when
|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|
is large, and they provide a bias-corrected estimator for
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
that achieves the minimax rate (up to some logarithmic factor), provided the noise variance is known
a priori
. While their work demonstrated that it is possible to obtain minimax-optimal estimators of linear functions of eigenvectors, in this work we go one step further and analyze the distributional fluctuations of these estimators. We provide data-driven confidence intervals for
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
, and our results hold under nearly optimal signal-to-noise ratio conditions such that consistent eigenspace estimation is possible. In addition, our estimators can be computed directly from the data without the need for sample splitting.

In the following subsections we first describe the two models studied in this work, and we then describe the primary contributions of this paper in greater detail.

1.1
Two Canonical Settings

Throughout this work we focus on two fundamental statistical models: matrix denoising and principal component analysis.

•

Matrix Denoising
. Consider the setting where one is interested in the eigenvectors of some symmetric rank
r
matrix
\bm{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
corrupted with some symmetric Gaussian noise matrix
\bm{N}
, where
\bm{N}_{ij}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
for
i<j
and
\bm{N}_{ii}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,2\sigma^{2})
are independent (i.e.,
\bm{N}
belongs to the
Gaussian Orthogonal Ensemble
). Given a pre-specified deterministic vector
\bm{a}
, in this paper we consider estimating
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
, where
\bm{u}_{j}
is the eigenvector associated to the
j
’th largest in magnitude eigenvalue of
\bm{S}
.

•

Principal Component Analysis
. Suppose one has
n
i.i.d. observations
\bm{X}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
with
\bm{X}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\bm{\Sigma})
, where
\bm{\Sigma}
belongs to the class of
spiked covariance matrices
; i.e.,
\bm{\Sigma}=\bm{\Sigma}_{0}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{p}
, where
\bm{\Sigma}_{0}
is a positive semidefinite rank
r
matrix and
\bm{I}_{p}
denotes the
p\times p
identity. As in matrix denoising we consider estimating
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
for some pre-specified unit vector
\bm{a}
, where
\bm{u}_{j}
is the
j
’th eigenvector of
\bm{\Sigma}_{0}
(and hence also
\bm{\Sigma}
).

These models have been studied explicitly in the literature previously. For example,
Bao et al. 2021
;
Knowles and Yin 2014
;
Xia 2021

study the asymptotic properties of empirical eigenvectors and singular vectors, and

Bao et al. 2022
;
Koltchinskii and Lounici 2016
;
Koltchinskii et al. 2020
study the asymptotic properties of principal components. However, unlike the aforecited works, our emphasis is on providing finite-sample distributional guarantees and data-driven uncertainty quantification in regimes where the eigengaps (see
Section

2
for the formal definition) can be significantly smaller than considered in previous works.

Both of these models were studied in
Li et al. 2022
who also consider estimating
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
. However, unlike their work, in this work we focus on distributional theory and statistical inference for estimators of
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
, which requires novel analysis.

1.2
Contributions

Throughout this work we provide analogous results for both matrix denoising and principal component analysis. Our main contributions are as follows:

•

We establish nonasymptotic distributional theory in the form of Berry-Esseen theorems for linear functions of eigenvectors
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
in the presence of small eigengaps about the quantity
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}\bm{u}_{j}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
, which is a biased centering term. We then provide similar results for a bias-corrected estimator about the quantity
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
, where the bias correction is computed directly from data assuming knowledge of the noise variance
\sigma^{2}
. Our Berry-Esseen bounds clearly demonstrate the effect of bias, signal strength, and small eigengaps on the rate of convergence. As a corollary we provide entrywise distributional theory for individual eigenvectors.

•

We then study the problem of statistical inference for
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
, and we provide fully data-driven estimators for the bias and variance without assuming knowledge of the noise variance
\sigma^{2}
. We show that these estimators yield approximately valid confidence intervals, and we apply these results to obtain approximately valid uncertainty quantification for entries of individual eigenvectors.

Unlike previous work, our procedures are fully data-driven and do not require any sample splitting, and to the best of our knowledge, these results are the first to provide valid confidence intervals for linear functions of eigenvectors in these settings under nearly optimal signal-to-noise ratio conditions without any sample splitting. It is worth emphasizing that our results continue to hold even in the “large eigengaps” regime, and our distributional results reveal a novel phenomenon on the role of “close eigenvalues” in the rate of convergence. Our proofs are based on higher-order asymptotic expansions that allow us to identify leading-order terms and isolate several residual terms that are closely related to certain “eigenvalue bias” quantities. Together with several deterministic matrix-analytic results, the form of the residual terms allow us to decouple statistical randomness even when eigengaps may be small, enabling the sharp concentration required to prove our main results.

1.3
Organization

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In
Section

2
we formally define our models as well as several fundamental quantities that play a key role in our theory. In
Section

3
we state our distributional theory results for both the biased and unbiased estimators, and
Section

4
we describe our inferential procedures and their associated theoretical guarantees. We cover previous work in
Section

5
, we provide numerical simulations in
Section

6
, and we discuss in
Section

7
. The proofs of our main distributional theory and statistical inference results are given in
Section

8
. Proofs of additional corollaries and lemmas are deferred to the appendices.

1.4
Notation

We let
[n]
denote the set of integers
\{1,\dots,n\}
. For two numbers
a
and
b
we write
a\wedge b
to mean the minimum of
a
and
b
and
a\vee b
as the maximum. For two sequences
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
, we write
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
or
a_{n}=O(b_{n})
if there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that
a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}
, and we write
a_{n}\ll b_{n}
if
a_{n}/b_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. We write
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
and if
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
and
b_{n}\lesssim a_{n}
. We set
a_{n}=\Omega(b_{n})
if
b_{n}=O(a_{n})
. We write
a_{n}=\widetilde{O}(b_{n})
if there exists some
c>0
not depending on
n
such that
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}\log^{c}(n)
, and similarly for
a_{n}=\widetilde{\Omega}(b_{n})
. For a matrix
\bm{U}
with orthonormal columns we write
\bm{U}_{\perp}
to denote the (not necessarily unique) matrix satisfying
\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{U}=0
such that
[\bm{U},\bm{U}_{\perp}]
is an orthogonal matrix. We write
\bm{I}_{n}
for the
n\times n
identity matrix,
\|\cdot\|
as the spectral norm for matrices and Euclidean norm for vectors,
\|\cdot\|_{F}
as the Frobenius norm on matrices, and we let
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
denote the
\ell_{\infty}
norm on vectors. We let
\bm{e}_{i}
denote the
i
’th standard basis vector whose dimension will be clear from context. For a given vector
\bm{v}
, we let
\bm{v}(i)
denote its
i
’th entry. For a random variable
X
, we let
\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}
be its subgaussian Orlicz norm defined via
\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}:=\inf\{t>0:\mathbb{E}\exp(X^{2}/t^{2})\leq 2\}
.

2
Statistical Models and Key Quantities

In this section we formulate our two statistical models and introduce several important quantities that are particularly relevant in our main results.

2.1
Matrix Denoising

Suppose one is given a matrix
\bm{\widehat{S}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
of the form

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{S}}

\displaystyle=\bm{S}+\bm{N}

(1)

where
\bm{S}
is a rank
r
signal matrix with eigendecomposition given by

\displaystyle\bm{S}

\displaystyle=\bm{U}\bm{\Lambda}\bm{U}^{\top}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{u}_{i}^{\top},

and
\bm{N}
is a symmetric noise matrix with independent entries given by

\displaystyle\bm{N}_{ij}\sim\begin{cases}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})&i<j,\\
\mathcal{N}(0,2\sigma^{2})&i=j.\end{cases}

The matrix
\bm{N}
is said to belong to the
Gaussian Orthogonal Ensemble
(GOE). A key property of the GOE is that it is rotationally invariant; i.e., given a matrix
\bm{R}
satisfying
\bm{RR}^{\top}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}=\bm{I}_{n}
, it holds that
\bm{R}\bm{N}\bm{R}^{\top}
has the same distribution as
\bm{N}
.

We define

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}=\min_{1\leq i\leq r}|\lambda_{i}|;\qquad\lambda_{\max}=\max_{1\leq i\leq r}|\lambda_{i}|,

representing the magnitude of the smallest and largest nonzero eigenvalues of
\bm{S}
, and we set
\kappa
as the
reduced condition number

\kappa=\lambda_{\max}/\lambda_{\min}
. Similarly, we let
\bm{\widehat{S}}
have eigenvectors
\bm{\widehat{u}}_{k}
and eigenvalues
\widehat{\lambda}_{k}
. For a given index
j
with
1\leq j\leq r
, we define the eigenvalue gap (or eigengap) via

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}

\displaystyle=\min_{k\neq j,1\leq k\leq r}|\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}|,

with the convention that
\Delta_{j}=\lambda_{\min}
if
r=1
. We also set

\displaystyle\Delta_{\min}=\min_{1\leq j\leq r}\Delta_{j}.

We let
\bm{\widehat{U}}
denote the
n\times r
matrix of leading eigenvectors of
\bm{\widehat{S}}
organized according to the magnitude of the eigenvalues of
\bm{\widehat{S}}
. We set
\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
to be the
j
’th column of
\bm{\widehat{U}}
.

We will use the following setting as a running example.

Example 1

(Gaussian Mixed-Membership Blockmodel)
.

Consider the case that one is given a matrix
\bm{S}
of the form

\displaystyle\bm{S}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\bm{Z}\bm{B}\bm{Z}^{\top},

where
\bm{Z}\in[0,1]^{n\times 2}
has rows that sum to one and
\bm{B}=\bm{V}\mathrm{Diag}(\lambda+\varepsilon,\lambda)\bm{V}^{\top}
for some
2\times 2
orthonormal matrix
\bm{V}
. Here the matrix
\frac{1}{n}\bm{B}
is interpreted as a matrix of “community means,” with
\bm{Z}
playing the role of the “membership matrix,” where each row of
\bm{Z}
denotes the membership of the
i
’th vertex. The assumption that each row of
\bm{Z}
sums to one corresponds to a setting wherein each vertex belongs to a convex combination of communities, with “pure” membership corresponding to the row being strictly
\{0,1\}
-valued. It is straightforward to demonstrate that
\bm{S}
has matrix of eigenvectors equal to

\displaystyle\bm{U}

\displaystyle=\bm{Z}(\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z})^{-1/2}\bm{V}.

Furthermore, the smallest eigenvalue of
\bm{B}
is of order
\lambda
as long as
\lambda_{\min}(\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z})\asymp n
, which can be interpreted as stating that each community is of “approximately equal size.” For concreteness, we will consider the following model. Suppose
\bm{Z}
is the matrix with the first
(1-\alpha)n/2
rows equal to
(1,0)
, the second
(1-\alpha)n/2
rows equal to
(0,1)
, and the final
\alpha n
rows equal to
(.5,.5)
. Then

\displaystyle\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}(1-\alpha)n/2&\frac{1}{4}\alpha n\\
\frac{1}{4}\alpha n&(1-\alpha)n/2\end{pmatrix},

so that
\lambda_{\min}(\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z})=n\big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3}{4}\alpha\big)
and
\lambda_{\max}(\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z})=n\big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4}\alpha\big)
. Consequently, if
\alpha=o(1)
, then
\lambda_{\min}(\bm{S})\gtrsim\lambda
and

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}=\lambda_{\max}(\bm{S})-\lambda_{\min}(\bm{S})=\frac{1}{n}\lambda_{\max}\big(\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z}\bm{B}\big)-\frac{1}{n}\lambda_{\min}\big(\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z}\bm{B}\big)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{100},

which holds as long as
\alpha\asymp c\frac{\varepsilon}{\lambda}
and
\varepsilon\ll\lambda
.

In this work we consider several related problems:

•

Distributional theory for
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
Suppose one is given a fixed (deterministic) vector
\bm{a}
satisfying
\|\bm{a}\|=1
.
Consider estimating
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
based on the observation
\bm{\widehat{S}}
for some
1\leq j\leq r
. We will study the approximate Gaussianity of
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
about a biased centering term of the form
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}\bm{u}_{j}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
, which necessitates some form of debiasing procedure.

•

Distributional theory for the debiased quantity:
We then study the approximate Gaussianity of the debiased quantity
\sqrt{1+b_{j}^{{\sf MD}}}\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
, where
b_{j}^{{\sf MD}}
is a debias parameter computed from the data assuming knowledge of the noise variance
\sigma^{2}
.

•

Entrywise Distributional Theory:
We also consider the particular case
\bm{a}=\bm{e}_{i}
, where
\bm{e}_{i}
is a standard basis vector, which corresponds to the
i
’th entry of
\bm{u}_{j}
. In this case, under
incoherence conditions
(see
Corollary

3.1
for the formal definition), debiasing is not necessary.

•

Uncertainty quantification:
In
Section

4
we study the problem of obtaining confidence intervals for
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
as well as entrywise confidence intervals without assuming knowledge of the noise variance
\sigma^{2}
.

2.2
Principal Component Analysis

Consider the setting where one is given
n
observations
\bm{X}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
satisfying

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\bm{\Sigma}),

(2)

where
\bm{\Sigma}
has the form

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}=\bm{\Sigma}_{0}+\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{p},

with

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}_{0}=\bm{U}\bm{\Lambda}\bm{U}^{\top}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}\bm{u}_{i}\bm{u}_{i}^{\top},

where each
\lambda_{i}
satisfies
0<\lambda_{r}\leq\lambda_{r-1}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{1}
. We denote

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}=\lambda_{r};\qquad\lambda_{\max}=\lambda_{1},

and we define
\kappa
as the
reduced condition number

\kappa:=\lambda_{\max}/\lambda_{\min}
. Similar to matrix denoising, for a given index
j
with
1\leq j\leq r
, we denote the eigengap associated to the index
j
via

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}=\min_{k\neq j,1\leq k\leq r}|\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}|,

with
\Delta_{j}=\lambda_{\min}
if
r=1
. We also set

\displaystyle\Delta_{\min}=\min_{1\leq j\leq r}\Delta_{j}.

We consider estimating
\bm{\Sigma}
with the sample covariance
\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}
defined via

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{X}_{i}\bm{X}_{i}^{\top}=\frac{\bm{XX}^{\top}}{n},

where
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}
is the matrix whose columns are the observations
\bm{X}_{i}
. We let
\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}
have eigenvectors and eigenvalues
\{\bm{\widehat{u}}_{k},\widehat{\lambda}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{p}
. We let
\bm{\widehat{U}}
denote the
p\times r
matrix of leading eigenvectors of
\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}
, and we set
\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
to be the
j
’th column of
\bm{\widehat{U}}
.

For concreteness is it useful to think of the following two settings.

Example 2

(“Diagonal“ versus “Dense” Covariance)
.

Suppose that
\bm{\Sigma}_{0}
is the
p\times p
matrix with first two diagonal entries equal to
\lambda+\varepsilon
and
\lambda
respectively, and all others zero. Then
\bm{u}_{i}=\bm{e}_{i}
, and
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{\min}=\varepsilon
. We refer to this as the
diagonal covariance model
.

Now suppose that that
p
is even and suppose further that
\bm{\Sigma}_{0}=(\lambda+\varepsilon)\bm{u}_{1}\bm{u}_{1}^{\top}+\lambda\bm{u}_{2}\bm{u}_{2}^{\top}
, where

\bm{u}_{1}
is the vector with entries all equal to
\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}
and
\bm{u}_{2}
is the vector with entries
\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}
such that
\bm{u}_{1}^{\top}\bm{u}_{2}=0
. Then one again has
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{\min}=\varepsilon
. In this setting observe that
\bm{\Sigma}_{0}
is a dense block matrix, and we refer to this as the
dense covariance model
.

As in the case for matrix denoising, we consider several related problems:

•

Distributional theory for
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
and its debiased counterpart:
Suppose one is given a fixed (deterministic) vector
\bm{a}
satisfying
\|\bm{a}\|=1
, and consider estimating
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
based on the observation
\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}
for some
1\leq j\leq r
. We will study the approximate Gaussianity of
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
about the biased centering term of the form
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}\bm{u}_{j}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
, as well as the approximate Gaussianity of the debiased quantity
\sqrt{1+b_{j}^{{\sf PCA}}}\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
, where
b_{j}^{{\sf PCA}}
is a bias parameter computed from the data assuming knowledge of the noise variance
\sigma^{2}
.

•

Entrywise distributional theory and uncertainty quantification:
We further consider the entrywise distribution of
\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
, and we show that debiasing is not strictly necessary in this setting. In addition, in
Section

4
we study how to obtain asymptotically valid confidence intervals for
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
as well as entrywise confidence intervals without assuming knowledge of the noise variance
\sigma^{2}
.

3
Distributional Theory

With our models defined we are now prepared to discuss our first results, namely, our Berry-Esseen bounds. In
Section

3.1
we study matrix denoising and in
Section

3.2
we study PCA.

Throughout all of our results, we note that there is an eigenvector ambiguity issue since the sign of an eigenvector can always be flipped. We therefore assume without loss of generality that
\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
is chosen such that
\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
is nonnegative.

3.1
Matrix Denoising

The following result provides a distributional characterization for the plug-in estimator
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
.

Theorem 3.1

(Approximate Gausssianity for Linear Functions of Eigenvectors)
.

Consider the model (
1
), and suppose
\lambda_{j}
is unique. Suppose that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}/\sigma\geq C_{0}\sqrt{rn}\qquad\Delta_{j}/\sigma\geq C_{0}r\log^{2}(n)

where
C_{0}
is some sufficiently large constant. Suppose further that
r\leq c_{0}n/\log^{2}(n)
, where
c_{0}
is a sufficiently small constant. Let
\bm{a}
be any determinstic unit vector such that
\bm{a}\neq\pm\bm{u}_{j}
. Let
\bm{U}^{(k)}
denote the matrix whose columns are the eigenvectors corresponding to
\lambda_{k}
. Define

\displaystyle(s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}})^{2}\coloneqq 2\sum_{k\neq j,k\leq r^{\prime}}\frac{\sigma^{2}\|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{U}^{(k)}\|^{2}}{(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k})^{2}}+2\frac{\sigma^{2}\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|^{2}}{\lambda_{j}^{2}},

where the summation is over all
r^{\prime}\leq r
distinct eigenvalues.
Let
\Phi(z)
denote the cumulative distribution function of a standard Gaussian random variable. Then it holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\frac{1}{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}}}\big(\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}-\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}\bm{u}_{j}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}\big)\leq z\bigg\}-\Phi(z)\bigg|

\displaystyle\lesssim{\sf ErrMD}

where

\displaystyle{\sf ErrMD}:=\underbrace{\frac{r\sqrt{n\log(n)}}{\lambda_{\min}/\sigma}}_{\text{Subspace Estimation Effect}}+\underbrace{\frac{r^{3/2}\log(n)}{\Delta_{j}/\sigma}}_{\text{Small Eigengaps Effect}}+\ n^{-9}.

The assumption that
\bm{a}\neq\pm\bm{u}_{j}
is necessary, since otherwise
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}-\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}\bm{u}_{j}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}\equiv 0
and
s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}}=0
, so the limiting distribution may no longer be Gaussian.
Therefore, this condition is not particularly stringent. In addition, the factor
n^{-9}
can be replaced with any negative power of
n
at the cost of increasing the implicit constants, so in general it can be ignored.

Remark 3.1

(Signal-Strength Condition)
.

Observe that the bound in
Theorem

3.1
is only non-vacuous if
\lambda_{\min}/\sigma\gg r\sqrt{n\log(n)}
. It has been demonstrated in
Cai and Zhang 2018
that
\lambda_{\min}/\sigma\gg\sqrt{rn}
is both neccessary and sufficient for consistent subspace estimation in
\|\cdot\|_{F}
when
r\ll n
; consequently the condition
\lambda_{\min}/\sigma\gg r\sqrt{n\log(n)}
is optimal up to the factor of
\sqrt{r\log(n)}
.

Remark 3.2

(Eigengap Conditions)
.

Again the bound is only non-vacuous if
\Delta_{j}/\sigma\gg r^{3/2}\log(n)
. Together with our assumption on the eigengap
\Delta_{j}/\sigma\geq C_{0}r\log^{2}(n)
, we see that a sufficient condition for normality is
\Delta_{j}/\sigma\gg r^{3/2}\log^{2}(n)
. However, this eigengap condition is significantly weaker than what is required in classical theory. For example, the Davis-Kahan Theorem requires that

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}\gtrsim\|\bm{N}\|\asymp\sigma\sqrt{n},

which is more stringent by a factor of
\widetilde{\Omega}\big(\sqrt{\frac{n}{r^{3}}}\big)
.

Remark 3.3

(Rate of Convergence)
.

Ignoring the additional factor of
n^{-9}
, when
r\asymp 1
, the rate of convergence to asymptotic normality is of the form

\displaystyle\max\bigg\{\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\lambda_{\min}/\sigma},\frac{1}{\Delta_{j}/\sigma}\bigg\},

ignoring logarithmic terms, where we dub the first term as the “subspace estimation effect” and the second term as the “small eigengaps effect.”
Consequently,
Theorem

3.1
reveals a novel phenomenon – when the signal strength is large but eigengaps are small, the primary difficulty in estimation is separating the
j
’th eigenvector from the other leading
r-1
eigenvectors, whereas when the eigengaps are large but the signal strength is small, the primary difficulty lies in estimating the subspace
\bm{U}
, which corresponds to separating the leading
r
eigenvectors from the bottom
n-r
eigenvectors.

Remark 3.4

(Biased Estimation)
.

Observe that the centering term in
Theorem

3.1
is given by
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}\bm{u}_{j}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
. In general
\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
need not equal one; in fact, in the regime
\lambda_{\min}/\sigma\gg\sqrt{nr}
it can be shown that
\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}=1-o(1)
and when
\lambda_{\min}/\sigma\asymp\sqrt{nr}
then
\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}\approx c<1
(e.g.,
Benaych-Georges and
Nadakuditi 2011
). Consequently,
Theorem

3.1
demonstrates that
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
is a
biased
estimate of
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
with bias given by
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}(1-\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j})
, a phenomenon that has also been demonstrated for asymmetric noise in
Chen et al. 2021a
. When
|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|
is sufficiently small relative to
s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}}
this bias can be negligible (as we demonstrate in the proof
Corollary

3.1
, which focuses on the case
\bm{a}=\bm{e}_{i}
), but when
|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|\asymp 1
, this bias may be significant.

Remark 3.5

(Comparison to Previous Works)
.

Our work is most similar to the works
Li et al. 2022
and
Koltchinskii and Xia 2016
, who study perturbation bounds for the estimator
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
in similar contexts, and our assumptions on the signal strength
\lambda_{\min}/\sigma
match theirs up to logarithmic terms and factors of
r
. However, only
Li et al. 2022
consider the small eigengaps regime. Our condition on
\Delta_{j}
matches theirs up to logarithmic terms and factors of
r
, while simultaneously providing a sharper characterization of the estimator
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
in terms of nonasymptotic distributional theory.

In order to prove our results we must must identify the leading-order term, compute its variance
(s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}})^{2}
, and then demonstrate that the residual terms are of order
o(s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}})
. In contrast, both of these previous works only needed to show that the difference is of order
O(s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}})
. Therefore, our analysis must extend further (implicitly considering higher-order terms) to show that these residuals are all sufficiently small even with small eigengaps. To accomplish this goal we leverage heavily two deterministic matrix analysis results (
Theorems

8.1
and
8.2
) that allow us to decouple several statistically dependent quantities as well as isolate certain “eigenvalue bias” terms (see
Lemma

8.2
). See
Section

8.2.3
for further details on how these eigenvalue bias terms arise in the analysis. Identifying how these eigenvalue bias terms arise plays a key role in providing the sharp concentration guarantees required to prove
Theorem

3.1
.

Remark 3.6

(Cosines)
.

Suppose
\bm{a}=\bm{u}_{l}
for some
l\neq j
with
l\leq r
, and suppose
\lambda_{l}
is unique. Then note that
(s_{\bm{u}_{l},j}^{{\sf MD}})^{2}=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{l})^{2}}
.
Theorem

3.1
demonstrates that as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\frac{|\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{l}|}{\sigma}\cos\theta(\bm{u}_{l},\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j})\to\mathcal{N}(0,1),

as long as
\lambda_{\min}/\sigma\gg r\sqrt{n\log(n)}
and
\Delta_{j}/\sigma\gg r^{3/2}\log(n)
. To the best of our knowledge, our result is the first establishing the convergence of the cosines between leading eigenvectors when the order of the eigengaps can be significantly smaller than the order of the spike.

Example 3

(Gaussian Mixed-Membership Blockmodel, Continued)
.

In the setting of
Example

1
consider the case
\varepsilon\asymp\sigma\mathrm{polylog}(n)
and
\lambda\asymp\sigma\sqrt{n}
. Then for any
\bm{a}
such that
\bm{a}\neq\pm\bm{u}_{j}
, it holds that
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}-\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}\bm{u}_{j}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
is asymptotically Gaussian. In particular, consider testing whether
\alpha=0
(i.e., testing whether all the vertices are “pure” or not). If
\alpha=0
, it holds that
\bm{u}_{1}
is the constant vector; and hence by taking
\bm{a}
as the constant vector it holds that
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{2}
is asymptotically Gaussian about zero if
\alpha=0
; otherwise
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{2}
is asymptotically Gaussian about some nonzero quantity.

Our next result provides distributional guarantees for a bias-corrected estimate of
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
assuming knowledge of the noise variance
\sigma^{2}
.

Theorem 3.2

(Asymptotic Normality of Debiased Estimators – Matrix Denoising)
.

Consider the setting of
Theorem

3.1
. Define

\displaystyle b_{j}^{{\sf MD}}:

\displaystyle=\sum_{k>r}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{(\widehat{\lambda}_{j}-\widehat{\lambda}_{k})^{2}},

where
\sigma^{2}
is assumed known and
\widehat{\lambda}_{k}
are the ordered empirical eigenvalues of
\bm{\widehat{S}}
.
Then it holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\frac{1}{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}}}\big(\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}\sqrt{1+b_{j}^{{\sf MD}}}-\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}\big)\leq z\bigg\}-\Phi(z)\bigg|

\displaystyle\lesssim{\sf ErrMD}+\frac{|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|}{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}}}{\sf ErrBiasMD},

where
{\sf ErrMD}
and
s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}}
are as in
Theorem

3.1
and

\displaystyle{\sf ErrBiasMD}:=\frac{\sigma^{2}r\log(n)}{\Delta_{j}^{2}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n\log(n)}}{\lambda_{j}^{2}}.

Remark 3.7

(Bias Effect)
.

Observe that
Theorem

3.2
exhibits an additional error reflecting the order of
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
, whereas
Theorem

3.1
does not depend on
|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|
except through the centering term. For simplicity assume that
\bm{S}
is positive semidefinite. Then it is straightforward to demonstrate that

\displaystyle\frac{|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|}{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}}}{\sf ErrBiasMD}\lesssim\frac{\sigma\sqrt{r}\log(n)}{\Delta_{j}}\min_{k\neq j,k\leq r}\frac{|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|}{|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{k}|}\frac{|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j}|}{\Delta_{j}}+\frac{|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|}{\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|}\frac{\sigma\sqrt{n\log(n)}}{\lambda_{j}}.

If it holds that
|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{k}|\asymp|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|
for all
k\neq j,k\leq r
and
|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j}|\asymp\Delta_{j}
for all
k\neq j,k\leq r
, then
Theorem

3.2
implies that

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\frac{1}{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}}}\big(\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}\sqrt{1+b_{j}^{{\sf MD}}}-\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}\big)\leq z\bigg\}-\Phi(z)\bigg|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{r\sqrt{n\log(n)}}{\lambda/\sigma}+\frac{r^{3/2}\log(n)}{\Delta_{j}/\sigma}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|}{\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|}\frac{\sigma\sqrt{n\log(n)}}{\lambda_{j}}+n^{-9}.

The additional term incurs a factor of
\frac{|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|}{\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|}
, which quantifies the effect of
\bm{a}
on the subspace corresponding to
\bm{u}_{j}
relative to the orthogonal complement of the leading subspace. Consequently, this result can be understood as stating that
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
cannot be “too close” to
\pm 1
to obtain asymptotic normality with this plug-in estimator. In contrast,
Theorem

3.1
allows
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
to be arbitrarily close (but not equal) to
\pm 1
to obtain asymptotic normality.

We now turn our attention to the entrywise distribution of the individual eigenvectors; i.e., when
\bm{a}=\bm{e}_{i}
. Under this setup, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 3.1

(Entrywise Distributional Theory – Matrix Denoising)
.

In the setting of
Theorem

3.2
, let
\bm{a}=\bm{e}_{i}
; i.e.,
\bm{a}
is a standard basis vector, and suppose that there exists a constant
\mu=O(1)
such that

\displaystyle\max_{k\leq r}|\bm{u}_{k}(i)|

\displaystyle\leq\mu\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}}.

Suppose further that

\max_{k\leq r,k\neq j}\frac{|\bm{u}_{j}(i)|}{|\bm{u}_{k}(i)|}=O(1).

Let
s^{{\sf MD}}_{\bm{e}_{i},j}
be as in
Theorem

3.1
.
Then it holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\frac{1}{s^{{\sf MD}}_{\bm{e}_{i},j}}\bigg(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}(i)-\bm{u}_{j}(i)\bigg)\leq z\bigg\}-\Phi(z)\bigg|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{r\sqrt{n\log(n)}}{\lambda_{\min}/\sigma}+\frac{r^{3/2}\log(n)}{\Delta_{j}/\sigma}+n^{-8}.

The proof of this result can be found in
Section

B.7
.

Remark 3.8

(Comparison to Previous Works)
.

The constant
\mu
plays the role of the
incoherence constant
that arises in a number of problems related to entrywise estimation. The works
Chen et al. 2021b
and
Agterberg et al. 2022
establish distributional theory for individual rows of
\bm{\widehat{U}}\mathcal{O}-\bm{U}
, where
\mathcal{O}
is an
r\times r
orthogonal transformation that arises due to eigenvalue multiplicity. Explicitly, a straightforward adaptation of their results yields that

\displaystyle\frac{|\bm{\Lambda}|}{\sigma}\bigg(\bm{\widehat{U}}\mathcal{O}-\bm{U}\bigg)_{i\cdot}\to\mathcal{N}(0,\bm{I}_{r}),

when
r,\kappa,\mu_{0}=O(1)
, provided certain signal-strength conditions are met. Therefore, these results imply that marginally

\displaystyle\frac{|\lambda_{j}|}{\sigma}\bigg(\bm{\widehat{U}}\mathcal{O}-\bm{U}\bigg)_{ij}\to\mathcal{N}(0,1).

(3)

Observe that
Corollary

3.1
and (
3
) are of a similar form: both results qualitatively provide the order of the fluctuations of entries of the
j
’th eigenvector. Note that these previous works do not assume any conditions on the eigengaps, in particular allowing eigenvalue multiplicity. However, in these settings their results can fail to yield the precise distribution of individual eigenvector entries when the eigengaps can be small, as (
3
) still contains an additional orthogonal transformation that cannot in general be eliminated (unless the eigengaps are sufficiently large). Therefore, our result complements these previous results as
Corollary

3.1
provides individual eigenvector entry fluctuations without assuming stringent conditions on the eigengaps. Our result can also be interpreted as a more “fine-grained” result isolating the effect of “nearby” eigenvectors (as quantified by eigengap) on individual eigenvectors.

Remark 3.9

(Extensions to Other “Negligible Bias” Regimes)
.

In
Corollary

3.1
we focus only on the entrywise distribution, where incoherence plays a crucial role providing the “unalignment” of
\bm{a}=\bm{e}_{i}
with
\bm{U}
.

Corollary

3.1
can be generalized to settings such that
\max_{k\leq r}|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{k}|\lesssim n^{-1/2}
with
|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|\lesssim|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{k}|
. In such a regime, bias correction is no longer necessary, as the bias is of order
n^{-1/2}
, and hence is negligible. However, such a result requires the
a priori
knowledge that
\|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{U}\|
is sufficiently small, which may not occur in practice without additional assumptions (such as incoherence).

3.2
Principal Component Analysis

In this section we provide analogous results to those in
Section

3.1
for principal component analysis. The following result provides an analogue of
Theorem

3.1
for the principal component analysis model (
2
).

Theorem 3.3

(Approximate Gaussianity of Linear Functions of Principal Components)
.

Consider the model in (
2
) and suppose
\lambda_{j}
is unique. Suppose that
r\leq c_{0}\frac{n}{\kappa^{2}\log^{4}(n\vee p)}
for some sufficiently small constant
c_{0}
, that
\log(p)\lesssim n
, and that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}

\displaystyle\geq C_{1}\max\bigg\{\sigma^{2}\log^{2}(n\vee p)\bigg(\frac{p}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\bigg),\kappa\log^{4}(n\vee p)\sigma\frac{p}{n}\Bigg\};

(4)

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}

\displaystyle\geq C_{1}\big(\lambda_{\max}+\sigma^{2}\big)\sqrt{\frac{r}{n}}\log(n\vee p),

where
C_{1}
is a sufficiently large constant. Let
\bm{a}
be any determinstic unit vector such that
\bm{a}\neq\pm\bm{u}_{j}
. Let
\bm{U}^{(k)}
denote the eigenspace corresponding to
\lambda_{k}
. Define

\displaystyle(s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf PCA}})^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\neq j,k\leq r^{\prime}}\frac{(\lambda_{j}+\sigma^{2})(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2})\|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{U}^{(k)}\|^{2}}{n(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k})^{2}}+\frac{(\lambda_{j}+\sigma^{2})\sigma^{2}\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|^{2}}{n\lambda_{j}^{2}},

where the summation is over all
r^{\prime}\leq r
distinct eigenvalues. Then it holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\frac{1}{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf PCA}}}\bigg(\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}-\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}\bm{u}_{j}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}\bigg)\leq z\bigg\}-\Phi(z)\bigg|

\displaystyle\lesssim{\sf ErrPCA},

where

\displaystyle{\sf ErrPCA}

\displaystyle:=\underbrace{\kappa^{3/2}r\log^{3}(n\vee p)\Bigg(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\lambda_{j}}\bigg(\frac{p}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(n\vee p)}{n}}\bigg)+\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\Bigg)}_{\text{Subspace Estimation Effect}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\kappa r^{5/2}\log^{5/2}(n\vee p)\frac{(\lambda_{\max}+\sigma^{2})}{\Delta_{j}\sqrt{n}}}_{\text{Small Eigengaps Effect}}+\underbrace{\kappa^{2}r^{3/2}\log^{5/2}(n\vee p)\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}}_{\text{Parametric Effect}}.

Remark 3.10

(Signal-Strength Condition)
.

Up to logarithmic terms, factors of
\kappa
, and factors of
r
, our assumption on
\lambda_{\min}
in (
4
) requires that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}/\sigma^{2}\gtrsim\max\bigg\{\frac{p}{n},\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}},\frac{1}{\sigma}\frac{p}{n}\bigg\},

which matches the assumption in
Li et al. 2022
. By
Cai et al. 2013
, the minimax rate of estimation for
\bm{U}
in Frobenius norm is of the form

\displaystyle\frac{pr}{n}\frac{(\lambda_{\min}/\sigma^{2})+1}{(\lambda_{\min}/\sigma^{2})^{2}}\asymp\frac{pr}{n}\max\bigg\{\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}/\sigma^{2}},\frac{1}{(\lambda_{\min}/\sigma^{2})^{2}}\bigg\}.

(5)

Therefore, when
\sigma\gtrsim\max\{\sqrt{\frac{n}{p}},1\}
, our assumption is optimal up to logarithmic terms. When
\sigma\ll\max\{\sqrt{\frac{n}{p}},1\}
, our condition is not optimal, but this regime falls under a setting where
\bm{\Sigma}
is close to rank degenerate, which is a simpler setting than when
\sigma
is larger. This regime may require a slightly different analysis to take into account the small amount of noise, but the main results will likely not materially change.

Remark 3.11

(Eigengap Conditions)
.

In order for the bound to be non-vacuous, when
\kappa=O(1)
, our result requires
\Delta_{j}\gtrsim(\lambda_{\max}+\sigma^{2})\frac{r^{5/2}\log^{5/2}(n\vee p)}{\sqrt{n}}
. In contrast, classical theory such as the Davis-Kahan Theorem implicitly requires that

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}\asymp(\lambda_{\max}+\sigma^{2}),

when
r,\kappa=O(1)
. Consequently, when
r,\kappa=O(1)
and
\log(p)\ll n
, our assumption is significantly weaker by a factor of
\widetilde{\Omega}(n^{-1/2})
, while still being amenable to the setting
\Delta_{j}\asymp(\lambda_{\max}+\sigma^{2})
.

Remark 3.12

(Biased Estimation)
.

As in matrix denoising, the centering term in
Theorem

3.3
is given by
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}\bm{u}_{j}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
. In previous work (e.g.,
Paul 2007
) it has been established that
\bm{\widehat{u}}_{1}^{\top}\bm{u}_{1}\to c\in(0,1)
when
\lambda_{\min}/\sigma^{2}\asymp 1
and
p/n
converges to a constant (provided
\lambda_{\min}/\sigma^{2}
is above a certain threshold), and
\bm{\widehat{u}}_{1}^{\top}\bm{u}_{1}=1-o(1)
when
\lambda_{\min}/\sigma^{2}
grows sufficiently quickly. Therefore,
Theorem

3.3
demonstrates that
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}
is a biased estimate of
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
, with bias given by
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}(1-\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j})
. In
Theorem

3.4
we will study the effect of bias estimation on the distributional theory.

Remark 3.13

(Rate of Convergence)
.

When
\kappa,r=O(1)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\frac{1}{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf PCA}}}

\displaystyle\bigg(\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}-\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}\bm{u}_{j}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}\bigg)\leq z\bigg\}-\Phi(z)\bigg|

\displaystyle=\widetilde{O}\Bigg\{\frac{(\lambda_{\max}+\sigma^{2})}{\Delta_{j}\sqrt{n}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\lambda_{j}}\bigg(\frac{p}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\bigg)+\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\Bigg\}.

Therefore, the rate becomes

\displaystyle\max\Bigg\{\frac{(\lambda_{\max}+\sigma^{2})}{\Delta_{j}\sqrt{n}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\lambda_{j}}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}},\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\Bigg\},

ignoring logarithmic terms. Similar to the matrix denoising setting, the quantities
\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\lambda_{j}}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}
and
\frac{\sigma}{\lambda_{j}^{1/2}}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}
quantify the “subspace estimation effect,” or how easy it is to separate the leading
r
principal components from the remaining components, where the two different regimes arise according to the relationship between
\lambda/\sigma^{2}
and
\frac{p}{n}
(see the minimax rate given in (
5
)), and the quantity
\frac{\lambda_{\max}+\sigma^{2}}{\Delta_{j}\sqrt{n}}
quantifies the “small eigengaps effect” via how well it is to separate the
j
’th eigenvector from the other eigenvectors. Unlike matrix denoising, PCA exhibits an additional factor of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
, representing the classical parametric rate that cannot be overcome even when the noise is relatively small.

Remark 3.14

(Comparison to Previous Works)
.

Theorem

3.3
is most similar to the works
Koltchinskii and Lounici 2016
;
Koltchinskii et al. 2020
, both of whom study estimating
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
. In fact, in the particular setting that
\sigma^{2}=o\bigg(\lambda_{\min}\frac{n}{p\times\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\bigg)
, that
\lambda_{1}
through
\lambda_{r}
converge to some fixed distinct values, that
r=O(1)
, and that
\Delta_{j}\gtrsim\lambda_{\max}+\sigma^{2}
, our results resemble Theorem 7 of
Koltchinskii and Lounici 2016
. However, our eigengap condition is significantly weaker as mentioned above, and our results are nonasymptotic, thereby allowing
\kappa
and
r
to grow in
n
and
p
. Furthermore, our asymptotic variance matches the variance obtained in
Koltchinskii et al. 2020
, who demonstrate that this variance is optimal over a sufficiently broad class of covariance matrices
\bm{\Sigma}
. Therefore,
Theorem

3.3
shows that the empirical estimator is
biased
, but
asymptotically efficient
.

Our proof techniques are also significantly different from these previous works. In
Koltchinskii and Lounici 2016
and
Koltchinskii et al. 2020
the authors use tools from holomorphic functional calculus to develop their main results; however, when the eigengaps are permitted to be small these techniques are no longer available. A key feature of the holomorphic functional calculus is that it automatically yields the form of the leading-order term that gives rise to the asymptotic normality. Therefore, in our results we must identify the leading-order term directly and show that the remaining terms are sufficiently small relative to the leading-order standard deviation
s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf PCA}}
.

In addition, our assumption on
\Delta_{j}
is comparable to that in
Li et al. 2022
. However, while
Li et al. 2022
need only show that the error is of order
O(s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf PCA}})
, we must demonstrate that the residual terms are of order
o(s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf PCA}})
. As in matrix denoising, we find it useful to identify a certain form of “eigenvalue bias” (see
Lemma

8.9
) that allows us to provide sharp bounds on residual terms. This characterization together with our careful residual expansion allows us to provide the stronger guarantees required to obtain
Theorem

3.3
.

Remark 3.15

(Cosines)
.

Assuming that
\kappa,r=O(1)
, take
\bm{a}=\bm{u}_{l}
for
l\neq j
with
l\leq r
in
Theorem

3.3
to reveal that (assuming
\lambda_{l}
is unique)

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\frac{|\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{l}|}{\sqrt{(\lambda_{j}+\sigma^{2})(\lambda_{l}+\sigma^{2})}}\cos\theta(\bm{u}_{l},\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j})\to\mathcal{N}(0,1),

as long as
\lambda_{\min}
satisfies

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}/\sigma^{2}\gg\widetilde{\Omega}\bigg(\max\bigg\{\frac{p}{n},\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}},\frac{1}{\sigma}\frac{p}{n}\bigg\}\bigg)

and
\Delta_{j}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}\gg(\lambda_{\max}+\sigma^{2})\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}.

To the best of our knowledge this is the first result establishing the asymptotic Gaussianity of the cosines of empirical principal components in the small eigengaps regime.

Example 4

(“Diagonal” versus “Dense” Covariance, Continued)
.

Suppose that
\bm{\Sigma}
is the diagonal covariance matrix in
Example

2
. Then
(s_{\bm{a},1}^{{\sf PCA}})^{2}
simplifies to

\displaystyle(s_{\bm{a},1}^{{\sf PCA}})^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\lambda+\varepsilon+\sigma^{2})(\lambda+\sigma^{2})|\bm{a}(2)|^{2}}{n\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{(\lambda+\varepsilon+\sigma^{2})\sigma^{2}\|\bm{a}_{-1-2}\|^{2}}{n(\lambda+\varepsilon)^{2}},

hwere
\bm{a}_{-1-2}
denotes the vector
\bm{a}
with its first two coordinates removed. The expression above reveals how the “spread” of the entries of
\bm{a}
affects the asymptotic variance, where less concentration of the magnitude of
\bm{a}
in the first two entries corresponds to a smaller asymptotic variance. Furthermore, as
\varepsilon\to 0
with
\bm{a}
held fixed, the asymptotic variance increases.

Now suppose that
\bm{\Sigma}
is the “dense” covariance, and suppose that
\bm{a}
has its first
p/2
entries nonnegative and its second
p/2
entries nonpositive. Then

\displaystyle(s_{\bm{a},1}^{{\sf PCA}})^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\lambda+\varepsilon+\sigma^{2})(\lambda+\sigma^{2})\|\bm{a}\|_{1}^{2}}{np\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{(\lambda+\varepsilon+\sigma^{2})\sigma^{2}\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|^{2}}{n(\lambda+\varepsilon)^{2}}.

If
\|\bm{a}\|_{1}\asymp 1
(e.g., if
\bm{a}
is sufficiently sparse), then the second term can dominate the first term for
p
sufficiently large. However, if
\|\bm{a}\|_{1}\asymp\sqrt{p}
(e.g., if
\bm{a}
is sufficiently dense with the magnitude of its entries approximately equally spread), then the first term can dominate.

These two examples showcase how manifestly different phenomena result from both the unit vector
\bm{a}
and the underlying covariance matrix. Such a result is reminiscent of the setting of statistical inference for linear functions of high-dimensional coefficients in linear regression, where different rates appear based on the sparsity of the linear function
(
Cai and Guo 2017
)
.

We next consider the approximate Gaussianity of the debiased estimator proposed in
Li et al. 2022
, assuming knowledge of the noise variance
\sigma^{2}
.

Theorem 3.4

(Approximate Gaussianity of Debiased Estimator – PCA)
.

Suppose the conditions of
Theorem

3.3
hold. Define the quantity
b_{j}^{{\sf PCA}}
via

\displaystyle b_{j}^{{\sf PCA}}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\dfrac{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}}{n+\sum_{r<i\leq n}\frac{\widehat{\lambda}_{i}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}-\widehat{\lambda}_{i}}}{\sum}_{i:r<i\leq n}\dfrac{\widehat{\lambda}_{i}}{(\widehat{\lambda}_{j}-\widehat{\lambda}_{i})^{2}}&n\geq p;\\
\dfrac{\frac{\sigma^{2}p}{n}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}-\frac{\sigma^{2}p}{n}}+\dfrac{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}-\frac{\sigma^{2}p}{n}}\dfrac{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}}{n+\sum_{i:r<i\leq n}\frac{\widehat{\lambda}_{i}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}-\widehat{\lambda}_{i}}}{\sum}_{i:r<i\leq n}\dfrac{\widehat{\lambda}_{i}-\frac{\sigma^{2}p}{n}}{(\widehat{\lambda}_{j}-\widehat{\lambda}_{i})^{2}}&n<p.\end{cases}

Then it holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\frac{1}{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf PCA}}}\bigg(\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}\sqrt{1+b_{j}^{{\sf PCA}}}-\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}\bigg)\leq z\bigg\}-\Phi(z)\bigg|

\displaystyle\lesssim{\sf ErrPCA}+\frac{|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|}{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf PCA}}}{\sf ErrBiasPCA},

where
{\sf ErrPCA}
and
s^{{\sf PCA}}_{\bm{a},j}
are defined in
Theorem

3.3
, and
{\sf ErrBiasPCA}
is defined via

\displaystyle{\sf ErrBiasPCA}

\displaystyle:=\frac{(\lambda_{\max}+\sigma^{2})(\lambda_{j}+\sigma^{2})r\log(n\vee p)}{\Delta_{j}^{2}n}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\sigma(\lambda_{j}+\sigma^{2})^{1/2}}{\lambda_{j}\sqrt{n}}\Bigg(\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r}\log^{2}(n\vee p)}{\lambda_{\min}^{1/2}}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{r\kappa}\log^{2}(n\vee p)}{\lambda_{\min}}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\Bigg)

Remark 3.16

(Bias Effect)
.

As in matrix denoising,
Theorem

3.4
incurs an additional “debiasing effect” pertaining to the magnitude of
|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|
, whereas
Theorem

3.3
does not depend on
|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|
except through the centering term. It is possible to demonstrate that

\displaystyle\frac{|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|}{s^{{\sf PCA}}_{\bm{a},j}}{\sf ErrBiasPCA}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{(\lambda_{\max}+\sigma^{2})\sqrt{r}\kappa\log(n\vee p)}{\Delta_{j}\sqrt{n}}\min_{k\neq j,k\leq r}\frac{|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|}{|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{k}|}\frac{|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j}|}{\Delta_{j}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|}{\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|}\Bigg(\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r}\log^{2}(n\vee p)}{\lambda_{\min}^{1/2}}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{r\kappa}\log^{2}(n\vee p)}{\lambda_{\min}}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\Bigg),

where the term in parentheses is
o(1)
under our noise assumption (
4
). This additional quantity reflects the effect of
\bm{a}
on the subspace corresponding to
\bm{u}_{j}
relative to the other subspaces.

Finally we consider the entrywise distribution of individual eigenvectors. The following result provides an analogue of
Corollary

3.1
for PCA. The proof can be found in
Section

C.8
.

Corollary 3.2

(Entrywise Distributional Theory)
.

In the setting of
Theorem

3.2
, let
\bm{a}=\bm{e}_{i}
; i.e.
\bm{a}
is a standard basis vector, and suppose there exists a constant
\mu=O(1)
such that

\displaystyle\max_{k\leq r}|\bm{u}_{k}(i)|\leq\mu\sqrt{\frac{1}{p}}.

Suppose further that

\displaystyle\max_{k\neq j,k\leq r}\frac{|\bm{u}_{j}(i)|}{|\bm{u}_{k}(i)|}=O(1).

Let
s^{{\sf PCA}}_{\bm{e}_{i},j}
be as in
Theorem

3.3
.
Then it holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\frac{1}{s^{{\sf PCA}}_{\bm{e}_{i},j}}\bigg(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}(i)-\bm{u}_{j}(i)\bigg)\leq z\bigg\}-\Phi(z)\bigg|\lesssim{\sf ErrPCA},

where
{\sf ErrPCA}
is defined in
Theorem

3.3
.

Just as in the case of matrix denoising, the form of
\bm{s}_{\bm{e}_{i},j}^{{\sf PCA}}
characterizes the effect of other eigenvectors and eigenvalues on the asymptotic variance, whereas previous works (e.g.,
Yan et al. 2021
) only hold up to some nonidentifiable orthogonal transformation.

Note that
Corollary

4.2
holds for the “dense” covariance model discussed in
Example

2
, but it does not hold for the “diagonal” covariance model.

4
Statistical Inference

While the results in
Section

3
yield approximate Gaussianity for the bias-corrected estimator for
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
, they fall short of providing the guarantees required to quantify the uncertainty in the estimate. In this section we consider estimation and inference procedures that result in approximately valid coverage.

4.1
Matrix Denoising

Recall that
Theorem

3.2
requires knowledge of the noise variance
\sigma^{2}
which is typically unknown in practice. We therefore define the following estimator of
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
, defined via

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}^{2}:

\displaystyle=\binom{n}{2}^{-1}\|\mathcal{P}_{{\sf upper-diag}}\big(\bm{\widehat{S}}-\bm{\widehat{S}}_{r}\big)\|_{F}^{2},

(6)

where
\bm{\widehat{S}}_{r}
is the best rank
r
approximation of
\bm{\widehat{S}}
, and where
\mathcal{P}_{{\sf upper-diag}}
denotes the projection onto the upper off-diagonal. Using this estimator of the noise variance we can reliably estimate
b_{j}^{{\sf MD}}
with
\widehat{b_{j}^{{\sf MD}}}
, defined via

\displaystyle\widehat{b_{j}^{{\sf MD}}}:=\sum_{k>r}\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{(\widehat{\lambda}_{j}-\widehat{\lambda}_{k})^{2}}.

(7)

Next, when the eigenvalues are distinct, recall that
s^{{\sf MD}}_{\bm{a},j}
is defined via

\displaystyle\big(s^{{\sf MD}}_{\bm{a},j}\big)^{2}=2\sum_{k\neq j,k\leq r}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j})^{2}}(\bm{u}_{k}^{\top}\bm{a})^{2}+2\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\lambda_{j}^{2}}\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|^{2}.

There are several quantities that require estimating; the first is
\sigma^{2}
, for which we use
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
above. Another key quantity that requires estimating is
(\bm{u}_{k}^{\top}\bm{a})^{2}
for
k\neq j
; however, by the theory in the previous section the plug-in estimator
\bm{\widehat{u}}_{k}^{\top}\bm{a}
will be biased for
\bm{u}_{k}^{\top}\bm{a}
. Therefore, instead of using
(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{k}^{\top}\bm{a})^{2}
for our estimate of
(\bm{u}_{k}^{\top}\bm{a})^{2}
, we propose to use
(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{k}^{\top}\bm{a})^{2}(1+\widehat{b_{k}^{{\sf MD}}})
, where
\widehat{b_{k}^{{\sf MD}}}
is the estimate of the debiasing parameter for the
k
’th eigenvector obtained via
Eq.

7
.

Finally,
s^{{\sf MD}}_{\bm{a},j}
also requires estimating the leading
r
eigenvalues. Given
k\leq r
, a natural estimate for
\lambda_{k}
is the empirical eigenvalue
\widehat{\lambda}_{k}
; however, our analysis (see
Lemma

8.2
) demonstrates that
\widehat{\lambda}_{k}
is a biased estimate of
\lambda_{k}
. Therefore, motivated by the form of this eigenvalue bias, we propose to use a debiased estimate of
\lambda_{k}
, defined via

\displaystyle\widecheck{\lambda}_{k}:=\widehat{\lambda}_{k}-\sum_{i>r}\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{k}-\widehat{\lambda}_{i}}.

With all these individual estimators in place, we then define

\displaystyle\big(\widehat{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}}}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{k\neq j,k\leq r}\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{(\widecheck{\lambda}_{j}-\widecheck{\lambda}_{k})^{2}}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{k}^{\top}\bm{a})^{2}(1+\widehat{b_{k}^{{\sf MD}}})+2\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{\widecheck{\lambda}_{j}^{2}}\|\bm{\widehat{U}}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|^{2}.

This estimator is entirely data-driven and does not require any sample-splitting. With this estimator we can define a
1-\alpha
confidence interval for
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
; the full procedure is summarized in
Algorithm

1
. The following result demonstrates the approximate validity of the resulting confidence intervals.

0:

Input matrix
\bm{\widehat{S}}
, rank
r
, index
j\in[r]
, unit vector
\bm{a}
, desired confidence level
\alpha
.

1:

Compute the eigendecomposition of
\bm{\widehat{S}}=\big[\bm{\widehat{U}},\bm{\widehat{U}}_{\perp}\big]\bm{\widehat{\Lambda}}\big[\bm{\widehat{U}},\bm{\widehat{U}}_{\perp}\big]^{\top}
, where
\bm{\widehat{U}}
has columns consisting of the
r
eigenvectors corresponding to the largest in magnitude nonzero eigenvalues. Let

\bm{\widehat{u}}_{k}
denote the
k
’th column of
\bm{\widehat{U}}
and
\widehat{\lambda}_{k}
denote the
k
’th largest in magnitude eigenvalue of
\bm{\widehat{S}}
.

2:

Set
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
via
Eq.

6
.

3:

Set
\widehat{b_{k}^{{\sf MD}}}
via
Eq.

7
for
1\leq k\leq r
.

4:

Compute, for
1\leq k\leq r
,

\displaystyle\widecheck{\lambda}_{k}:=\widehat{\lambda}_{k}-\sum_{i>r}\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{k}-\widehat{\lambda}_{i}}.

5:

Set

\displaystyle\big(\widehat{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}}}\big)^{2}:=2\sum_{k\leq r,k\neq j}\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{(\widecheck{\lambda}_{j}-\widecheck{\lambda}_{k})^{2}}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{k}^{\top}\bm{a})^{2}(1+\widehat{b_{k}^{{\sf MD}}})+2\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{\widecheck{\lambda}_{j}^{2}}\|\bm{\widehat{U}}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|^{2}.

6:

Compute
{\sf C.I.}^{(\alpha)}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}^{\top}\bm{a})
defined via

\displaystyle{\sf C.I.}^{(\alpha)}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}^{\top}\bm{a}):=\bigg[\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}^{\top}\bm{a}\sqrt{1+\widehat{b_{j}^{{\sf MD}}}}\pm\Phi^{-1}\big(1-\alpha/2\big)\widehat{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}}}\bigg],

where
\Phi^{-1}
is the inverse of the cumulative density function for a standard Gaussian random variable.

7:

return
Estimated confidence interval
{\sf C.I.}^{(\alpha)}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j},\bm{a})
.

Algorithm 1

Confidence Interval for
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
– Matrix Denoising

Theorem 4.1

(Validity of Confidence Intervals for Matrix Denoising)
.

Consider the model (
1
), and suppose
\lambda_{1}
through
\lambda_{r}
are unique. Suppose further that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}/\sigma\geq C_{0}\kappa^{2}r\sqrt{n}\log^{3/2}(n);\qquad\Delta_{\min}/\sigma\geq C_{0}r^{3/2}\log^{5/2}(n)

for some sufficiently large constant
C_{0}
.
In addition, assume that
r\leq c_{0}\frac{n}{\kappa^{2}\log^{2}(n)}
, where
c_{0}
is a sufficiently small constant. Suppose further that

\displaystyle\max_{k\neq j,k\leq r}\frac{s_{\bm{a},k}^{{\sf MD}}}{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}}}\frac{\sigma\sqrt{r}\log(n)}{\Delta_{j}}=o(1).

Furthermore, assume that

\displaystyle\frac{|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|}{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}}}\bigg(\frac{\sigma^{2}r\log(n)}{\Delta_{j}^{2}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}\kappa\sqrt{rn\log(n)}}{\lambda_{\min}^{2}}\bigg)=o(1).

Let
{\sf C.I.}^{(\alpha)}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}^{\top}\bm{a})
denote the output of
Algorithm

1
. If either
\|\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{a}\|>\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|
with
|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|\lesssim|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{k}|
for
k\neq j,k\leq r
or
\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|\geq\|\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{a}\|
, it holds that

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\bm{u}_{j}^{\top}\bm{a}\in{\sf C.I.}^{(\alpha)}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}^{\top}\bm{a})\bigg\}-(1-\alpha)\bigg|=o(1).

To the best of our knowledge,
Theorem

4.1
is the first result in the literature providing asymptotically valid confidence intervals for linear functions of eigenvectors in the matrix denoising context considered herein. Moreover, our results allow
r
and
\kappa
to grow with
n
.

Remark 4.1

(Generality)
.

En route to the proof of
Theorem

4.1
we actually prove a significantly more general, albeit more complicated, result in the form of a Berry-Esseen bound similar to
Theorem

3.2
. In particular, we show that

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\frac{\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}\sqrt{1+\widehat{b_{j}^{{\sf MD}}}}-\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}}{\widehat{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}}}}\leq z\bigg\}-\Phi(z)\bigg|\leq\zeta,

where
\zeta
is the quantity in
Lemma

8.6
. In
Lemma

8.6
we demonstrate that the conditions of
Theorem

4.1
imply that
\zeta=o(1)
.

Remark 4.2

(Alignment of
\bm{a}
)
.

Observe that
Theorem

4.1
requires different assumptions based on how well
\bm{a}
is aligned with the leading eigenspace: if
\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|<\|\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{a}\|
, we further assume that
|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|\lesssim|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{k}|
, whereas if
\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|\geq\|\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{a}\|
we make no further assumptions. To understand the intuition behind this condition, when
\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|<\|\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{a}\|
, the vector
\bm{a}
is more closely aligned with the leading eigenspace, and hence we must ensure that
\bm{a}
does not fall “too much” into the subspace spanned by
\bm{u}_{j}
(relative to
\bm{u}_{k}
for
k\leq r
). When
\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|\geq\|\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{a}\|
, the vector
\bm{a}
lies primarily in the subspace spanned by
\bm{U}_{\perp}
, and we need not make any further assumptions (beyond the assumptions already made).

Remark 4.3

(Eigengap Conditions)
.

Note that
Theorem

4.1
requires a condition on the minimum eigengap
\Delta_{\min}
, wheras
Theorems

3.1
and
3.2
only impose conditions on
\Delta_{j}
. In essence, this extra assumption is imposed to ensure a faithful estimate of
s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}}
, which requires estimating
(\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{k})^{2}
for all
k\leq r
with
k\neq j
, and hence our estimation of these quantities will only be uniformly guaranteed when
\Delta_{\min}
is sufficiently large. Nonetheless, this condition on
\Delta_{\min}
is still significantly weaker than required by classical theory. It may be possible to extend the result to settings with repeated eigenvalues assuming
a priori
knowledge of which eigenvalues are repeated.

Example 5

(Gaussian Mixed-Membership Blockmodel, Continued)
.

In the setting of
Example

1
again consider the case
\varepsilon\asymp\sigma\mathrm{polylog}(n)
and
\lambda\asymp\sigma\sqrt{n}\mathrm{polylog}(n)
. Then to test whether
\alpha=0
one may form a confidence interval using
Algorithm

1
with
\bm{a}
equal to the constant vector and rejecting if
0
is not in the interval. Under the null hypothesis
\alpha=0
, this test is consistent, and under local alternatives such that
\frac{|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|}{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf MD}}}\gg 1
, this test will have power converging to one. Further analysis is needed for when such local alternatives hold, but we leave this to future work.

Finally we return to the entrywise setting considered in
Corollary

3.1
, where we recall that no debiasing parameter is needed. The following result demonstrates the validity of entrywise confidence intervals obtained without the extra debiasing step under a slightly different condition than given in
Theorem

4.1
. The proof of this result can be found in
Section

B.8
.

Corollary 4.1

(Entrywise Confidence Intervals – Matrix Denoising)
.

Consider the model (
1
) and suppose
\lambda_{1}
through
\lambda_{r}
are unique. Suppose further that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}\geq C_{0}\sigma r\sqrt{n}\kappa^{2}\log^{3/2}(n);\qquad\Delta_{\min}\geq C_{0}\sigma r^{3/2}\kappa\log^{5/2}(n)

for some sufficiently large constant
C_{0}
. In addition, assume that
r\leq c_{0}\frac{n}{\kappa^{2}\log^{2}(n)}
, where
c_{0}
is a sufficiently small constant. Assume that there exists a constant
\mu=O(1)
such that

\displaystyle\max_{k\leq r}|\bm{u}_{k}(i)|\leq\mu\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}}.

Suppose further that

\max_{k,l\leq r}\frac{|\bm{u}_{l}(i)|}{|\bm{u}_{k}(i)|}=O(1).

Let
{\sf C.I.}^{(\alpha)}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}(i))
denote the output of
Algorithm

2
. Then it holds that

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\bm{u}_{j}(i)\in{\sf C.I.}^{(\alpha)}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}(i))\bigg\}-(1-\alpha)\bigg|=o(1).

In essence,
Corollary

4.1
delivers valid entrywise confidence intervals for empirical eigenvectors when the magnitude of the entries of each of the leading eigenvectors are of similar order. Existing work has primarily delivered
row-wise
confidence intervals for eigenspaces, and hence our result can be understood as a more “fine-grained” result providing confidence intervals for entries of individual eigenvectors while still allowing for the setting where eigengaps can be small. While we assume that
\mu=O(1)
it is possible that the analysis can be extended to the setting
\mu=o(\sqrt{n})
, but we leave this for future work.

0:

Input matrix
\bm{\widehat{S}}
, rank
r
, index
j\in[r]
, index
i\in[n]
, desired confidence level
\alpha
.

1:

Compute the eigendecomposition of
\bm{\widehat{S}}=\big[\bm{\widehat{U}},\bm{\widehat{U}}_{\perp}\big]\bm{\widehat{\Lambda}}\big[\bm{\widehat{U}},\bm{\widehat{U}}_{\perp}\big]^{\top}
, where
\bm{\widehat{U}}
has columns consisting of the
r
eigenvectors corresponding to the largest in magnitude nonzero eigenvalues. Let

\bm{\widehat{u}}_{k}
denote the
k
’th column of
\bm{\widehat{U}}
and
\widehat{\lambda}_{k}
denote the
k
’th largest in magnitude eigenvalue of
\bm{\widehat{S}}
.

2:

Set
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
via
Eq.

6
.

3:

Set
\widehat{b_{k}^{{\sf MD}}}
via
Eq.

7
for
1\leq k\leq r
,
k\neq j
.

4:

Compute, for
1\leq k\leq r
,

\displaystyle\widecheck{\lambda}_{k}:=\widehat{\lambda}_{k}-\sum_{i>r}\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{k}-\widehat{\lambda}_{i}}.

5:

Set

\displaystyle\big(\widehat{s_{\bm{e}_{i},j}^{{\sf MD}}}\big)^{2}:=2\sum_{k\leq r,k\neq j}\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{(\widecheck{\lambda}_{j}-\widecheck{\lambda}_{k})^{2}}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{k}(i))^{2}(1+\widehat{b_{k}^{{\sf MD}}})+2\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{\widecheck{\lambda}_{j}^{2}}\|\bm{\widehat{U}}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{e}_{i}\|^{2}.

6:

Compute
{\sf C.I.}^{(\alpha)}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}(i))
defined via

\displaystyle{\sf C.I.}^{(\alpha)}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}(i)):=\bigg[\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}(i)\pm\Phi^{-1}\big(1-\alpha/2\big)\widehat{s_{\bm{e}_{i},j}^{{\sf MD}}}\bigg],

where
\Phi^{-1}
is the inverse of the cumulative density function for a standard Gaussian random variable.

7:

return
Estimated confidence interval
{\sf C.I.}^{(\alpha)}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}(i))
.

Algorithm 2

Entrywise Confidence Intervals – Matrix Denoising

4.2
Principal Component Analysis

Similar to the matrix denoising setting, in
Theorem

3.4
we assume that the noise variance
\sigma^{2}
is assumed known, which is not true in practice. We therefore consider two different estimators based on the aspect ratio
\frac{p}{n}
. Define

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}^{2}:=\begin{cases}\dfrac{{\sf Tr}\big(\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}-\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{r}\big)}{p-r}&p\geq\dfrac{n}{\log^{4}(n\vee p)};\\
\widehat{\lambda}_{r+1}&p<\dfrac{n}{\log^{4}(n\vee p)}.\end{cases}

(8)

The intuition of this estimator can be understood as follows: when
p\ll n
, the eigenvalues of
\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}
are already consistent for those of
\bm{\Sigma}
, and hence
\widehat{\lambda}_{r+1}
will approximate
\sigma^{2}
. However, when
p
is relatively large, the approximation fails, and a more careful approximation must be done by leveraging the independence of the noise, which becomes more effective when
p
is large. The choice of the cutoff
n/\log^{4}(n\vee p)
is primarily theoretical; it is possible that a similar argument will work with any choice for
p
strictly smaller than
n
.

With the estimator
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
defined, we can also define the estimated debiasing parameter
\widehat{b_{j}^{{\sf PCA}}}
,defined via

\displaystyle\widehat{b_{j}^{{\sf PCA}}}:=\begin{cases}\dfrac{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}}{n+\sum_{r<i\leq n}\frac{\widehat{\lambda}_{i}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}-\widehat{\lambda}_{i}}}{\sum}_{i:r<i\leq n}\dfrac{\widehat{\lambda}_{i}}{(\widehat{\lambda}_{j}-\widehat{\lambda}_{i})^{2}}&n\geq p;\\
\dfrac{\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}p}{n}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}p}{n}}+\dfrac{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}p}{n}}\dfrac{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}}{n+\sum_{i:r<i\leq n}\frac{\widehat{\lambda}_{i}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}-\widehat{\lambda}_{i}}}{\sum}_{i:r<i\leq n}\dfrac{\widehat{\lambda}_{i}-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}p}{n}}{(\widehat{\lambda}_{j}-\widehat{\lambda}_{i})^{2}}&n<p.\end{cases}

(9)

Next, recall that

\displaystyle(s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf PCA}})^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\neq j,k\leq r}\frac{(\lambda_{j}+\sigma^{2})(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2})(\bm{u}_{k}^{\top}\bm{a})^{2}}{n(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k})^{2}}+\frac{(\lambda_{j}+\sigma^{2})\sigma^{2}\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|^{2}}{n\lambda_{j}^{2}}.

As in the matrix denoising setting, estimating the asymptotic variance requires estimating
(\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{k})
for
k\leq r
and
\lambda_{k}
for
k\leq r
. As will be made clear in our analysis (and also observed in
Li et al. 2022
), the empirical eigenvalues
\widehat{\lambda}_{j}
exhibit a multiplicative bias (see
Lemma

8.9
). Therefore, we propose to use a debiased estimator for
\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}
, defined via:

\displaystyle\widecheck{\lambda}_{k}:

\displaystyle=\frac{\widehat{\lambda}_{k}}{1+\widehat{\gamma}^{{\sf PCA}}(\widehat{\lambda}_{k})},

where

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}^{{\sf PCA}}(\widehat{\lambda}_{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=r+1}^{\min(p-r,n)}\frac{\widehat{\lambda}_{i}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{k}-\widehat{\lambda}_{i}}.

Under our assumptions we can demonstrate that
\widecheck{\lambda}_{k}
is a strong estimator of
\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}
. To estimate
\lambda_{j}^{2}
, we simply use the estimator
\widecheck{\lambda}_{j}-\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
. If
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\approx\sigma^{2}
, then it holds that
\widecheck{\lambda}_{j}-\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\approx\lambda_{j}
.

Finally, in the asymptotic variance we also need to estimate
(\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{k})^{2}
for
k\leq r
and
k\neq j
.
As in the matrix denoising case, the plug-in estimators for
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{k}
may not be sufficiently close to merit a strong estimate. We therefore propose to use the debiasing parameter
\widehat{b_{k}^{{\sf PCA}}}
to form the estimator
(\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{k})^{2}(1+\widehat{b_{k}^{{\sf PCA}}})
, which, from the previous analysis, is a strong estimator of
(\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{k})^{2}
under conditions on
\Delta_{\min}
.

Combining these estimators, we set

\displaystyle\big(\widehat{s^{{\sf PCA}}_{\bm{a},j}}\big)^{2}:

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\neq j}\frac{\widecheck{\lambda}_{k}\widecheck{\lambda}_{j}(\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{k})^{2}\big(1+\widehat{b_{k}^{{\sf PCA}}}\big)}{(\widecheck{\lambda}_{j}-\widecheck{\lambda}_{k})^{2}}+\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}(\widecheck{\lambda}_{j})\|\bm{\widehat{U}}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|^{2}}{(\widecheck{\lambda}_{j}-\widehat{\sigma}^{2})^{2}},

and we use this estimator to construct a
1-\alpha
confidence interval for
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
. The full procedure is summarized in
Algorithm

3
. The following result demonstrates the approximate validity of the resulting confidence intervals.

0:

Sample data
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}
, rank
r
, index
j\in[r]
, unit vector
\bm{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, desired confidence level
\alpha
.

1:

Compute the eigendecomposition of
\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}=\frac{\bm{XX}^{\top}}{n}=\big[\bm{\widehat{U}},\bm{\widehat{U}}_{\perp}\big]\bm{\widehat{\Lambda}}\big[\bm{\widehat{U}},\bm{\widehat{U}}_{\perp}\big]^{\top}
, where
\bm{\widehat{U}}
has columns consisting of the
r
eigenvectors corresponding to the largest nonzero eigenvalues. Let
\bm{\widehat{u}}_{k}
denote the
k
’th column of
\bm{\widehat{U}}
and
\widehat{\lambda}_{k}
denote the
k
’th largest eigenvalue of
\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}
.

2:

Set
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
via
Eq.

8
.

3:

Set
\widehat{b_{k}^{{\sf PCA}}}
via
Eq.

9
for
1\leq k\leq r
.

4:

Compute, for
1\leq k\leq r
,

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}^{{\sf PCA}}(\widehat{\lambda}_{k}):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=r+1}^{\min(p-r,n)}\frac{\widehat{\lambda}_{i}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{k}-\widehat{\lambda}_{i}}

5:

Compute, for
1\leq k\leq r
,

\displaystyle\widecheck{\lambda}_{k}:=\frac{\widehat{\lambda}_{k}}{1+\widehat{\gamma}^{{\sf PCA}}(\widehat{\lambda}_{k})}.

6:

Set

\displaystyle\big(\widehat{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf PCA}}}\big)^{2}:=\sum_{k\leq r,k\neq j}\frac{\widecheck{\lambda}_{k}\widecheck{\lambda}_{j}(\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{k})(1+\widehat{b_{k}^{{\sf PCA}}})}{n(\widecheck{\lambda}_{j}-\widecheck{\lambda}_{k})^{2}}+\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\widecheck{\lambda}_{j}\|\bm{\widehat{U}}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|^{2}}{(\widecheck{\lambda}_{j}-\widehat{\sigma}^{2})}.

7:

Compute
{\sf C.I.}^{(\alpha)}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}^{\top}\bm{a})
defined via

\displaystyle{\sf C.I.}^{(\alpha)}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}^{\top}\bm{a}):=\bigg[\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}^{\top}\bm{a}\sqrt{1+\widehat{b_{j}^{{\sf PCA}}}}\pm\Phi^{-1}\big(1-\alpha/2\big)\widehat{s^{{\sf PCA}}_{\bm{a},j}}\bigg]

8:

return
Estimated confidence interval
{\sf C.I.}^{(\alpha)}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}^{\top}\bm{a})
.

Algorithm 3

Confidence Interval for
\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}
– PCA

Theorem 4.2

(Validity of Confidence Intervals – PCA)
.

Consider the model in (
2
) and suppose
\lambda_{1}
through
\lambda_{r}
are unique. Suppose further that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}

\displaystyle\geq C_{1}\max\bigg\{\sigma^{2}\kappa^{3}r^{2}\log^{7}(n\vee p)\bigg(\frac{p}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\bigg),\sigma\kappa\log^{4}(n\vee p)\frac{p}{n}\bigg\};

\displaystyle\Delta_{\min}

\displaystyle\geq C_{1}\big(\lambda_{\max}+\sigma^{2})\frac{r^{5/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\log^{4}(n\vee p),

where
C_{1}
is some sufficiently large constant. In addition, assume that
r^{3}\leq c_{1}\frac{n}{\kappa^{6}\log^{12}(n\vee p)}
, where
c_{1}
is a sufficiently small constant, and that
\log(p)\lesssim n^{1/2}
. Suppose that

\displaystyle\max_{k\neq j,k\leq r}\frac{s_{\bm{a},k}^{{\sf PCA}}}{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf PCA}}}\frac{(\lambda_{\max}+\sigma^{2})\sqrt{r}\log^{2}(n\vee p)}{\Delta_{j}\sqrt{n}}=o(1).

Finally, assume that

\displaystyle\frac{|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|}{s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf PCA}}}

\displaystyle\Bigg[\frac{(\lambda_{\max}+\sigma^{2})(\lambda_{j}+\sigma^{2})r\log(n\vee p)}{\Delta_{j}^{2}n}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\sigma(\lambda_{j}+\sigma^{2})^{1/2}}{\lambda_{j}\sqrt{n}}\bigg(\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{r}\log^{2}(n\vee p)}{\lambda_{\min}^{1/2}}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{r\kappa}\log^{2}(n\vee p)}{\lambda_{\min}}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\sigma^{2}p}{\lambda_{\min}n}\frac{\sqrt{r}\kappa\sqrt{\log(n\vee p)}}{n}\Bigg]=o(1).

Then if either
\|\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{a}\|>\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|
with
|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}|\lesssim|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{k}|
for
k\neq j,k\leq r
or
\|\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{a}\|\leq\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{a}\|
, then it holds that

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{u}_{j}\in{\sf C.I.}^{(\alpha)}(\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j})\bigg\}-(1-\alpha)\bigg|=o(1).

The assumptions in
Theorem

4.2
are similar to those of
Theorem

4.1
. As in the setting therein, different alignment of the vector
\bm{a}
results in qualitatively different phenomena.

Remark 4.4

(Generality)
.

Similar to the matrix denoising setting, we first prove a more general result in the form of a Berry-Esseen bound. See
Lemma

8.10
for details.

Remark 4.5

(Optimality and Statistical Efficiency)
.

As proven in
Koltchinskii et al. 2020
, the limiting variance
(s_{\bm{a},j}^{{\sf PCA}})^{2}
is optimal for a broad class of covariance matrices with “large” eigengaps. As a result, our confidence intervals are effectively the shortest possible asymptotically valid confidence intervals for a subclass of the class of covariance matrices studied therein. Moreover, unlike
Koltchinskii et al. 2020
, our confidence intervals do not require
any
sample splitting, whereas
Koltchinskii et al. 2020
require separating some small (in
n
) sample to estimate the debiasing parameter. While theoretically both results demonstrate their respective asymptotic optimality, in practice any amount of sample splitting still results in a small loss of finite-sample efficiency.

Finally, we return to the entrywise context discussed in
Corollary

3.2
. The following result demonstrates the validity of entrywise confidence intervals under incoherence conditions. The proof can be found in
Section

C.9
.

Corollary 4.2

(Entrywise Confidence Intervals – PCA)
.

Consider the model (
2
) and suppose
\lambda_{1}
through
\lambda_{r}
are unique. Suppose further that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}

\displaystyle\geq C_{1}\max\bigg\{\sigma^{2}\kappa^{3}r^{2}\log^{7}(n\vee p)\bigg(\frac{p}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\bigg),\sigma\kappa\log^{4}(n\vee p)\frac{p}{n}\bigg\};

\displaystyle\Delta_{\min}

\displaystyle\geq C_{1}\big(\lambda_{\max}+\sigma^{2})\kappa^{3/2}\frac{r^{5/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\log^{4}(n\vee p),

where
C_{1}
is some sufficiently large constant. In addition, assume that
r^{3}\leq c_{1}\frac{n}{\kappa^{6}\log^{12}(n\vee p)}
, that
r\leq c_{1}p^{1/2}
, where
c_{1}
is a sufficiently small constant, and that
\log(p)\lesssim n^{1/2}
. Assume there exists a constant
\mu=O(1)
such that

\displaystyle\max_{k\leq r}|\bm{u}_{k}(i)|\leq\mu\sqrt{\frac{1}{p}}.

Suppose further that

\displaystyle\max_{k,l\leq r}\frac{|\bm{u}_{l}(i)|}{|\bm{u}_{k}(i)|}=O(1).

Let
{\sf C.I.}^{(\alpha)}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}(i))
denote the output of
Algorithm

4
. Then it holds that

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\bm{u}_{j}(i)\in{\sf C.I.}^{(\alpha)}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{j}(i))\bigg\}-(1-\alpha)\bigg|=o(1).

Concretely,
Corollary

4.2
enables one to obtain asymptotically valid entrywise confidence intervals when the corresponding entries of the leading eigenvectors are of similar order to each other.

0:

Sample data
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}
, rank
r
, index
j\in[r]
, unit vector
\bm{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, desired confidence level
\alpha
.

1:

Compute the eigendecomposition of
\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}=\frac{\bm{XX}^{\top}}{n}=\big[\bm{\widehat{U}},\bm{\widehat{U}}_{\perp}\big]\bm{\widehat{\Lambda}}\big[\bm{\widehat{U}},\bm{\widehat{U}}_{\perp}\big]^{\top}
, where
\bm{\widehat{U}}
has columns consisting of the
r
eigenvectors corresponding to the largest nonzero eigenvalues. Let
\bm{\widehat{u}}_{k}
denote the
k
’th column of
\bm{\widehat{U}}
and
\widehat{\lambda}_{k}
denote the
k
’th largest eigenvalue of
\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}
.

2:

Set
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
via
Eq.

8
.

3:

Set
\widehat{b_{k}^{{\sf PCA}}}
via
Eq.

9
for
1\leq k\leq r
,
k\neq j
.

4:

Compute, for
1\leq k\leq r
,
